People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, hello Doctor. Thank you so much for having me. It’s very nice to meet you. 

I, um, I hope I’m not taking up time from people who need it more, but... well, my family suggested I come talk to you. I just feel like I’m always up and moving. I mean, I know it’s a good thing to stay active at my age, right? I try to keep a positive attitude. But it's just... even on a quiet day at home, I find myself sort of bouncing from the garden club list, and then over to my knitting bag, and then suddenly I'm digging through travel papers... usually before I’ve really finished any one of those things. 

I just have a lot of energy and like to keep busy, I suppose. Does that make sense? I hope I'm explaining it alright for you.

Well, um, I suppose it's not entirely new. I mean, I've always been a bit of an energetic sort, you know? Even back when I was just a little girl in grade school... I remember my mother telling me that my teachers thought I was very bright. Which was lovely of them to say, of course! But they also mentioned I was a little... well, they used the word "scattered." 

I guess I had a bit of a habit of misplacing my school papers, and sometimes I'd get a little too eager and talk when I wasn't supposed to. And, oh, I was always wiggling around in my chair before my assignments were done. Just a busybody, really! Nothing terrible, I hope. It's just always been my way. Is that the sort of thing you were asking about, Doctor?

Oh, well... I wouldn't say it causes me *significant* distress, no. I mean, I certainly don't want to make a mountain out of a molehill! I know there are people dealing with far worse things than a silly old woman forgetting her schedule. 

I suppose it just... it makes me feel a little embarrassed, sometimes. When I see my husband sigh, or when the ladies at the center give each other that little look... I just hate feeling like a burden, you know? I want to be the one helping, not the one needing to be managed. But I try not to dwell on it too much. I just try to smile and do better next time. I hope that's the right attitude to have about it... do you think I'm worrying too much over nothing, Doctor?

activities.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S014\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Has this feeling of always being up and moving around, jumping from one thing to another — is this something new for you, or has it been the way you've always been for as long as you can remember?\n\nPatient: Well, um, I suppose it's not entirely new. I mean, I've always been a bit of an energetic sort, you know? Even back when I was just a little girl in grade school... I remember my mother telling me that my teachers thought I was very bright. Which was lovely of them to say, of course! I suppose, if I'm being perfectly honest, it has been causing a little bit of... friction lately. \n\nJust at home, mostly, and down at the community center. My husband, bless his heart, he gets a little frustrated sometimes because... well, he feels like he can't quite count on me to keep our plans straight. And the other volunteers at the center, I think they feel the same way. They're all so wonderful, but I know they've started sort of... double-checking behind me, just to make sure things get done. \n\nI feel just awful about it, really. I want to be the one helping, not the one needing to be managed. But I try not to dwell on it too much. I just try to smile and do better next time. Oh, goodness, getting worse? I don't know if I'd say *worse*, exactly. I mean, I'm certainly not falling apart at the seams! But, well... I suppose it has been a little more noticeable lately.

activities.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 70 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S014\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: You mentioned these things have been part of your personality since childhood. But I'm curious — have these symptoms ever caused you real problems? For instance, have they led to difficulties at work, in relationships, or with completing important responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... \"real problems\" might be a strong way to put it, I think. I mean, I'm certainly not trying to be difficult or anything! But... I suppose, if I'm being perfectly honest, it has been causing a little bit of... friction lately. \n\nJust at home, mostly, and down at the community center. My husband, bless his heart, he gets a little frustrated sometimes because... well, he feels like he can't quite count on me to keep our plans straight. They're all so wonderful, but I know they've started sort of... double-checking behind me, just to make sure things get done. \n\nI feel just awful about it, really. I really do try my best. Does that answer your question alright?\n\nDoctor: When you think about these things — the difficulty keeping plans straight, things slipping your mind, the double-checking others have to do — how much does that bother you personally? Does it cause you significant distress or worry?\n\nPatient: Oh, well... I wouldn't say it causes me *significant* distress, no. I mean, I certainly don't want to make a mountain out of a molehill! I know there are people dealing with far worse things than a silly old woman forgetting her schedule. \n\nI suppose it just... it makes me feel a little embarrassed, sometimes. When I see my husband sigh, or when the ladies at the center give each other that little look... I just hate feeling like a burden, you know? I want to be the one helping, not the one needing to be managed. But I try not to dwell on it too much. I just try to smile and do better next time. I hope that's the right attitude to have about it... do you think I'm worrying too much over nothing, Doctor?\n\nDoctor: You mentioned these things have been part of you since childhood. But I want to ask specifically about right now — in the past few weeks or months, have you noticed these symptoms getting worse, staying about the same, or actually improving compared to how they were a year or two ago?\n\nPatient: Oh, goodness, getting worse? I don't know if I'd say *worse*, exactly. I mean, I'm certainly not falling apart at the seams! But, well... I suppose it has been a little more noticeable lately. \n\nIf I had to put a timeline on it, I guess it's been a bit more pronounced for, oh, maybe a year and ten months or so? Just right around the time I offered to take on the extra coordinating duties at the community center. I just wanted to be helpful, you see! But with that, and all my other little activities... well, it's just been a bit more of a juggle than I anticipated. \n\nI really didn't mean to bite off more than I could chew, and I do hope I'm not making a mess of things for everyone else. It's just a bit much to keep track of sometimes. Does that make sense, Doctor? I hope I'm not complaining too much...\n\n[Last Question]\nAround the time these symptoms started getting more noticeable — about a year and ten months ago — did you experience anything significant health-wise? Oh, goodness, no. Nothing like that, thank heavens! I mean, I'm knocking on wood as I say it, but my health has been just fine. 

I haven't had any terrible falls or strange illnesses, and I certainly haven't started any new pills or anything of that sort. I just go for my regular check-ups, and my doctor always says I'm doing quite well for my age! I try to take good care of myself, you know, eat my vegetables and take a nice walk when the weather is agreeable. 

So, no, I don't think there was any big medical... event... or anything like that. Just me being my usual, slightly scattered self, I suppose, but with a few too many plates spinning at once. I hope that's the right kind of answer? I'd hate to think I was missing some important connection you were looking for, Doctor.

Oh, well... I wouldn't say it felt *overwhelming*, no. I certainly don't want to sound like I couldn't handle it! I was very happy to help out, truly I was. They needed someone, and I thought, well, I have the time, and it's for such a good cause.

Oh, well... I suppose if I'm being perfectly honest, I've always had a little trouble keeping my ducks in a row, even before the community center. I really don't want you to think my house is a disaster area, because I do keep it very clean! 

But... well, my kitchen table does tend to accumulate things. I've always had these little piles, you see. A pile for my dance class, one for my knitting patterns, another for the garden club events, and then folders for little trips I'd like to take... they just sort of live all over the table. And sometimes I stand there looking at all those lovely activities and I just can't quite figure out what needs to be done first. 

So it was definitely like that before, too, just... maybe a few less piles back then? I do hope that doesn't make me sound terribly messy, Doctor. I think a lot of ladies my age have a little clutter when they have active hobbies, wouldn't you say?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, I see what you mean. Well... I wouldn't say it's exactly worry or anxiety, no. I'm generally a very cheerful person! I don't sit around fretting, I really don't. 

It's more just... well, if I've been sitting there working on the garden club list for a little while, I start to get this... oh, I don't know, this slightly stirred-up feeling inside? It's not a bad feeling! Just a little... restless, I suppose you could say. 

Like, if I have to sit still for too long, my body just sort of tells me it's time to move. I'll get the urge to stand up and go straighten up the living room, or maybe go check on my knitting bag to see if I have the right yarn for a new pattern. It just feels nicer to be up and doing something else for a bit. 

I think that's quite normal though, isn't it? To want to stretch your legs and tidy up instead of sitting in one spot forever? I'd hate for you to think I'm bouncing off the walls or anything silly like that! I just like to keep busy, that's all. Does that sound like a reasonable explanation to you, Doctor?

I do hope that doesn't make me sound terribly messy, Doctor. I think a lot of ladies my age have a little clutter when they have active hobbies, wouldn't you say?\n\nDoctor: When you're in the middle of doing one of these activities — like working on the garden club list — do you find yourself suddenly *wanting* to jump to something else because it feels more exciting or interesting in that moment? Or is it more that you feel worried or anxious that you're not keeping up with everything, so you feel like you *need* to switch and check on the other things?\n\nPatient: Oh, I see what you mean. Well... I wouldn't say it's exactly worry or anxiety, no. I'm generally a very cheerful person! I don't sit around fretting, I really don't. \n\nIt's more just... well, if I've been sitting there working on the garden club list for a little while, I start to get this... oh, I don't know, this slightly stirred-up feeling inside? It's not a bad feeling! Just a little... restless, I suppose you could say. \n\nLike, if I have to sit still for too long, my body just sort of tells me it's time to move. I'll get the urge to stand up and go straighten up the living room, or maybe go check on my knitting bag to see if I have the right yarn for a new pattern. It just feels nicer to be up and doing something else for a bit. \n\nI think that's quite normal though, isn't it? To want to stretch your legs and tidy up instead of sitting in one spot forever? I'd hate for you to think I'm bouncing off the walls or anything silly like that! I just like to keep busy, that's all. Does that sound like a reasonable explanation to you, Doctor?\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you get that restless, stirred-up feeling and want to switch activities, does your mind feel like it's racing or jumping around quickly between different thoughts? Or does it feel more like your body just needs to move, even though your mind is calm?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, well... racing? No, I wouldn't say my mind is racing. That sounds so... frantic, doesn't it? I certainly don't feel frantic! My thoughts are usually quite pleasant, really. 

It's... it's just that little urge to move, like I mentioned. My body just says, "Alright, time to get up!" It's not that my mind is going a mile a minute. I just... I suppose I just think of something else I'd like to do, and I'd rather go do it than stay sitting. 

It's more of a physical need to stretch and be active, I think. I mean, my mind is perfectly fine. I just... I just like to be on my feet. That's a good thing, right? To stay active at my age? I really hope you don't think I'm losing my mind or anything like that, Doctor!

Oh, well... they are so very sweet to help, they really are. I certainly don't want to sound ungrateful! But... I suppose, if I'm being completely honest... yes, things do still manage to fall through the cracks sometimes.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: When you're in the middle of doing one of these activities — like working on the garden club list — do you find yourself suddenly *wanting* to jump to something else because it feels more exciting or interesting in that moment? Well... I wouldn't say it's exactly worry or anxiety, no. I'm generally a very cheerful person! I don't sit around fretting, I really don't. \n\nIt's more just... well, if I've been sitting there working on the garden club list for a little while, I start to get this... oh, I don't know, this slightly stirred-up feeling inside? People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, a specific example? Well... goodness. I... I really don't think anything terribly important has been completely missed. No, no. I mean, it's mostly just silly little things, you know? Like maybe forgetting to bring the extra paper napkins to the center, or... or misplacing a grocery list for an afternoon. Nothing major! 

I certainly wouldn't let a big, important responsibility just... fall away. I'd feel just awful if I did something like that! I'd hate for you to think I'm completely unreliable or that I'm ruining events. So, no, I can't really recall a specific time recently where something truly important didn't get done. Mostly because my husband and the ladies are so very good at catching my little slip-ups before they become real problems. 

That makes sense, doesn't it? I really do try my absolute best to keep the important things in order, I promise!

It just feels nicer to be up and doing something else for a bit. \n\nI think that's quite normal though, isn't it? To want to stretch your legs and tidy up instead of sitting in one spot forever?
